He began picking up extra shifts to cover medical expenses, then driving to Mexico on his days off to help coordinate her care, leaving little time for his usual outlets like car restoration or board‑game nights. Over several months, his wife noticed that his energy and focus around work and family swung dramatically: at times he was taking on new clients and home projects with almost aggressive zeal, insisting he could handle everything, and at other times he seemed paralyzed by worry and unable to organize basic tasks like paying bills or planning visits. The situation came to a head when he made several scheduling mistakes that jeopardized a client’s medication routine, prompting his supervisor to meet with him and suggest he see a mental health professional after Gustavo admitted that his mind felt scattered and he couldn’t keep his thoughts straight for more than a few minutes.\n- Chief Complaint: “I can’t keep my thoughts organized anymore, and it’s getting hard to make even simple decisions at work.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S024 (Depressed_Mood): Lately I wake up feeling this heavy, empty sadness, like a gray cloud sitting on my chest, and even when I’m with my wife or talking to my mom on the phone I just feel hollow inside and start tearing up for no clear reason.\n- S027 (Sleep_Disturbance): Most nights I either lie there for hours with my mind running through every possible way my mom’s health could go bad, or I jolt awake at three or four in the morning thinking I missed a client’s meds and can’t get back to sleep.\n- S029 (Fatigue): Even on days when I don’t have extra shifts, just showering, getting dressed, and driving to one client’s house wipes me out so much that I feel like I’ve already worked a double shift by noon.\n- S030 (Worthlessness_Guilt): I keep telling myself I’m a terrible son and husband, that if my mom loses her foot or one of my clients ends up in the hospital it’ll be completely my fault for not doing enough or messing up the schedule.\n- S031 (Diminished_Thinking): At work now I’ll open the med log, stare at it, and my brain just goes blank so I read the same line over and over and still can’t decide if I already gave the pills or not, which scares me because it never used to be this hard.\n- S032 (Death_Suicide): More and more I catch myself thinking it wouldn’t be so bad if I just didn’t wake up one day, or that everyone would be better off if I weren’t around screwing things up, even though I haven’t made any plans to do anything.\n- S033 (Elevated_Irritable_Mood): I swing from feeling weirdly amped up and snapping at people in traffic or at my wife over tiny things, to suddenly crashing into this low, defeated mood where I don’t want to talk to anyone at all.\n- S034 (Increased_Activity): On my high days I can’t sit still—I’ll clean the whole apartment, reorganize the pantry, start planning a new remodeling project for the house, and still feel like I need to be doing more, like I’m powered by a motor.\n- S035 (Inflated_Self_Esteem): There are times when I’m revved up that I honestly feel like I’m the only one who can keep my mom alive and handle all my clients better than any other aide, like I’m built to run on no sleep and fix everything myself.\n- S036 (Decreased_Sleep): When I’m in that wired mode, I’ll sleep maybe three or four hours, wake up before my alarm, and feel totally charged like I don’t need any more rest, so I just jump straight into making calls and planning things.\n- S039 (Manic_Distractibility): Lately I’ll be trying to fill out charting and every little thing pulls me away—the TV in the next room, a text ding, a car driving by—so I leave half-finished notes everywhere and keep bouncing from one thing to another without finishing.\n- S040 (Increased_Goal_Activity): In those driven stretches I start taking on extra clients, offering to fix things around their houses, texting relatives to organize my mom’s care, and even trying to line up side jobs, all at once, like I have to keep adding more or I’ll explode.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: Between the messed-up schedules at work and snapping at my wife when I’m in those revved-up phases, I’ve had clients reassigned, my supervisor warning me I could lose hours, and my wife taking over the bills and reminders because I keep dropping the ball.\n- duration: Looking back, these big swings between wired, overconfident periods and then crashing into weeks of feeling drained and hopeless have been happening off and on for a couple of years, but they’ve gotten a lot worse since my mom’s diabetes complications this past year.\n- depressive_episode_duration: This latest stretch of feeling down, guilty, and unable to think straight has been going on for about a month now, starting not long after my mom’s doctor said her diabetes had taken a turn for the worse.\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can I just know they definitely go on long enough to cause some major disruptions at my job and with my wife before I eventually crash.\n\nDoctor: During those 'wired' periods, have you ever experienced things that others couldn't see or hear, or held beliefs that were clearly not based in reality, such as feeling like you had special powers?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't have any supernatural powers. But when I'm wired, I do get this overwhelming feeling that I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm uniquely built to run on no sleep and handle all my clients better than anyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, do you feel like you are operating at a level that is clearly different from your usual self to the point where others notice a distinct change in your personality or behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's completely different from my normal self, and others definitely notice the change. My wife always points out these dramatic swings when I start taking on too many projects with aggressive zeal and snapping at her over tiny things. Even my supervisor noticed how scattered my behavior was after I made those scheduling mistakes that got me a warning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you are uniquely built to handle all your clients and save your mother, do you ever feel that these beliefs are so intense or unshakable that they might be considered delusional, or are you able to consider the possibility that you might be wrong?\n\nPatient: When I'm in those revved-up phases, that belief feels absolutely real and unshakable, like I'm a superhero who is the only one capable of saving my mom and handling everything. In the moment, I don't doubt myself at all and it doesn't feel like a delusion. But once I crash and look back on it later, I can clearly see how unrealistic and overwhelming those thoughts actually were."

But once I crash and look back on it later, I can clearly see how unrealistic and overwhelming those thoughts actually were.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, have you ever believed you possessed special, perhaps even supernatural, abilities or powers that others simply could not comprehend?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe I have any supernatural or magical powers. But during those wired periods, I do feel an almost superhuman sense of capability, truly believing I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm built to handle all my clients on almost no sleep."

Even my supervisor noticed how scattered my behavior was after I made those scheduling mistakes that got me a warning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you are uniquely built to handle all your clients and save your mother, do you ever feel that these beliefs are so intense or unshakable that they might be considered delusional, or are you able to consider the possibility that you might be wrong?\n\nPatient: When I'm in those revved-up phases, that belief feels absolutely real and unshakable, like I'm a superhero who is the only one capable of saving my mom and handling everything. In the moment, I don't doubt myself at all and it doesn't feel like a delusion. But once I crash and look back on it later, I can clearly see how unrealistic and overwhelming those thoughts actually were.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, have you ever believed you possessed special, perhaps even supernatural, abilities or powers that others simply could not comprehend?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe I have any supernatural or magical powers. But during those wired periods, I do feel an almost superhuman sense of capability, truly believing I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm built to handle all my clients on almost no sleep.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, has your belief in being uniquely capable ever reached a point where it was so fixed and out of touch with reality that others, such as your wife or doctor, would describe it as a delusion?\n\nPatient: I don't think it quite reached the level of a delusion or being completely detached from reality, but my wife definitely thought I was being highly unrealistic. During those times, I truly believed I was the only one who could keep my mom alive and handle all my clients on almost no sleep, which she saw as just extreme overconfidence about what I could manage alone."

I just know they definitely go on long enough to cause some major disruptions at my job and with my wife before I eventually crash.\n\nDoctor: During those 'wired' periods, have you ever experienced things that others couldn't see or hear, or held beliefs that were clearly not based in reality, such as feeling like you had special powers?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't have any supernatural powers. But when I'm wired, I do get this overwhelming feeling that I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm uniquely built to run on no sleep and handle all my clients better than anyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, do you feel like you are operating at a level that is clearly different from your usual self to the point where others notice a distinct change in your personality or behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's completely different from my normal self, and others definitely notice the change. My wife always points out these dramatic swings when I start taking on too many projects with aggressive zeal and snapping at her over tiny things. Even my supervisor noticed how scattered my behavior was after I made those scheduling mistakes that got me a warning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you are uniquely built to handle all your clients and save your mother, do you ever feel that these beliefs are so intense or unshakable that they might be considered delusional, or are you able to consider the possibility that you might be wrong?\n\nPatient: When I'm in those revved-up phases, that belief feels absolutely real and unshakable, like I'm a superhero who is the only one capable of saving my mom and handling everything. In the moment, I don't doubt myself at all and it doesn't feel like a delusion. But once I crash and look back on it later, I can clearly see how unrealistic and overwhelming those thoughts actually were.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, have you ever believed you possessed special, perhaps even supernatural, abilities or powers that others simply could not comprehend?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe I have any supernatural or magical powers. But during those wired periods, I do feel an almost superhuman sense of capability, truly believing I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm built to handle all my clients on almost no sleep.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, has your belief in being uniquely capable ever reached a point where it was so fixed and out of touch with reality that others, such as your wife or doctor, would describe it as a delusion?\n\nPatient: I don't think it quite reached the level of a delusion or being completely detached from reality, but my wife definitely thought I was being highly unrealistic. During those times, I truly believed I was the only one who could keep my mom alive and handle all my clients on almost no sleep, which she saw as just extreme overconfidence about what I could manage alone.\n\nDoctor: When you have these 'wired' periods that cause major problems at work and home, do they typically last for at least seven consecutive days, or do they usually subside in less than a week?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure of the exact number of days those wired periods last. They seem to go on for quite a while before I eventually crash, but I haven't tracked them closely enough to say if it's more or less than a week."

Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello Doctor, my name is Gustavo. I came in because I can’t keep my thoughts organized anymore, and it’s getting really hard to make even simple decisions at work lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of being overwhelmed and unable to focus before in your life, or is this a completely new experience for you?\n\nPatient: Actually, these big swings between feeling wired and overconfident, and then crashing into feeling completely drained and hopeless, have been happening off and on for a couple of years. It's not entirely new, but it has gotten much worse over the past year since my mom's health complications started.\n\nDoctor: During those periods when you feel 'wired and overconfident,' do you find that you need much less sleep than usual or that you engage in risky behaviors you wouldn't normally consider?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I'm in that wired mode, I'll sleep maybe three or four hours and wake up feeling totally charged, like I don't need any more rest at all. As for risky behaviors, I haven't really done anything like that, but I do tend to take on way too many home projects and extra work shifts all at once.\n\nDoctor: During those 'wired' periods, have these symptoms ever been severe enough to cause significant problems at work or in your relationships, or have you ever required hospitalization?\n\nPatient: I've never been hospitalized, but those wired times have definitely caused major problems at work and home. I've messed up client schedules so badly that some were reassigned, and my supervisor warned me I could lose my hours. At home, I snap at my wife, and she's had to take over managing the bills and daily reminders because I keep dropping the ball.\n\nDoctor: During these 'wired' periods that cause significant problems at work, do they typically last for at least one full week, or do they usually resolve within a few days?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact number of days they last, and I haven't really tracked the precise timing of those phases. I just know they definitely go on long enough to cause some major disruptions at my job and with my wife before I eventually crash.\n\nDoctor: During those 'wired' periods, have you ever experienced things that others couldn't see or hear, or held beliefs that were clearly not based in reality, such as feeling like you had special powers?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't have any supernatural powers. But when I'm wired, I do get this overwhelming feeling that I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm uniquely built to run on no sleep and handle all my clients better than anyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, do you feel like you are operating at a level that is clearly different from your usual self to the point where others notice a distinct change in your personality or behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's completely different from my normal self, and others definitely notice the change. My wife always points out these dramatic swings when I start taking on too many projects with aggressive zeal and snapping at her over tiny things. Even my supervisor noticed how scattered my behavior was after I made those scheduling mistakes that got me a warning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you are uniquely built to handle all your clients and save your mother, do you ever feel that these beliefs are so intense or unshakable that they might be considered delusional, or are you able to consider the possibility that you might be wrong?\n\nPatient: When I'm in those revved-up phases, that belief feels absolutely real and unshakable, like I'm a superhero who is the only one capable of saving my mom and handling everything. In the moment, I don't doubt myself at all and it doesn't feel like a delusion. But once I crash and look back on it later, I can clearly see how unrealistic and overwhelming those thoughts actually were.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, have you ever believed you possessed special, perhaps even supernatural, abilities or powers that others simply could not comprehend?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe I have any supernatural or magical powers. But during those wired periods, I do feel an almost superhuman sense of capability, truly believing I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm built to handle all my clients on almost no sleep.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, has your belief in being uniquely capable ever reached a point where it was so fixed and out of touch with reality that others, such as your wife or doctor, would describe it as a delusion?\n\nPatient: I don't think it quite reached the level of a delusion or being completely detached from reality, but my wife definitely thought I was being highly unrealistic. During those times, I truly believed I was the only one who could keep my mom alive and handle all my clients on almost no sleep, which she saw as just extreme overconfidence about what I could manage alone.\n\nDoctor: When you have these 'wired' periods that cause major problems at work and home, do they typically last for at least seven consecutive days, or do they usually subside in less than a week?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure of the exact number of days those wired periods last. They seem to go on for quite a while before I eventually crash, but I haven't tracked them closely enough to say if it's more or less than a week.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, do you find that they consistently last for at least one full week, or do they typically end before that seven-day mark?\n\nPatient: I'm not completely sure about the exact number of days those wired phases last. They definitely go on for several days at a time where I feel energized and barely sleep, but I've never kept close track of whether it's exactly a full week or slightly less."

They seem to go on for quite a while before I eventually crash, but I haven't tracked them closely enough to say if it's more or less than a week.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, do you find that they consistently last for at least one full week, or do they typically end before that seven-day mark?\n\nPatient: I'm not completely sure about the exact number of days those wired phases last. Could you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello Doctor, my name is Gustavo. I came in because I can’t keep my thoughts organized anymore, and it’s getting really hard to make even simple decisions at work lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of being overwhelmed and unable to focus before in your life, or is this a completely new experience for you?\n\nPatient: Actually, these big swings between feeling wired and overconfident, and then crashing into feeling completely drained and hopeless, have been happening off and on for a couple of years. It's not entirely new, but it has gotten much worse over the past year since my mom's health complications started.\n\nDoctor: During those periods when you feel 'wired and overconfident,' do you find that you need much less sleep than usual or that you engage in risky behaviors you wouldn't normally consider?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I'm in that wired mode, I'll sleep maybe three or four hours and wake up feeling totally charged, like I don't need any more rest at all. As for risky behaviors, I haven't really done anything like that, but I do tend to take on way too many home projects and extra work shifts all at once.\n\nDoctor: During those 'wired' periods, have these symptoms ever been severe enough to cause significant problems at work or in your relationships, or have you ever required hospitalization?\n\nPatient: I've never been hospitalized, but those wired times have definitely caused major problems at work and home. I've messed up client schedules so badly that some were reassigned, and my supervisor warned me I could lose my hours. At home, I snap at my wife, and she's had to take over managing the bills and daily reminders because I keep dropping the ball.\n\nDoctor: During these 'wired' periods that cause significant problems at work, do they typically last for at least one full week, or do they usually resolve within a few days?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure of the exact number of days they last, and I haven't really tracked the precise timing of those phases. I just know they definitely go on long enough to cause some major disruptions at my job and with my wife before I eventually crash.\n\nDoctor: During those 'wired' periods, have you ever experienced things that others couldn't see or hear, or held beliefs that were clearly not based in reality, such as feeling like you had special powers?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't have any supernatural powers. But when I'm wired, I do get this overwhelming feeling that I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm uniquely built to run on no sleep and handle all my clients better than anyone else.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, do you feel like you are operating at a level that is clearly different from your usual self to the point where others notice a distinct change in your personality or behavior?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's completely different from my normal self, and others definitely notice the change. My wife always points out these dramatic swings when I start taking on too many projects with aggressive zeal and snapping at her over tiny things. Even my supervisor noticed how scattered my behavior was after I made those scheduling mistakes that got me a warning.\n\nDoctor: When you feel like you are uniquely built to handle all your clients and save your mother, do you ever feel that these beliefs are so intense or unshakable that they might be considered delusional, or are you able to consider the possibility that you might be wrong?\n\nPatient: When I'm in those revved-up phases, that belief feels absolutely real and unshakable, like I'm a superhero who is the only one capable of saving my mom and handling everything. In the moment, I don't doubt myself at all and it doesn't feel like a delusion. But once I crash and look back on it later, I can clearly see how unrealistic and overwhelming those thoughts actually were.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, have you ever believed you possessed special, perhaps even supernatural, abilities or powers that others simply could not comprehend?\n\nPatient: No, I don't believe I have any supernatural or magical powers. But during those wired periods, I do feel an almost superhuman sense of capability, truly believing I'm the only one who can keep my mom alive and that I'm built to handle all my clients on almost no sleep.\n\nDoctor: During these intense periods, has your belief in being uniquely capable ever reached a point where it was so fixed and out of touch with reality that others, such as your wife or doctor, would describe it as a delusion?\n\nPatient: I don't think it quite reached the level of a delusion or being completely detached from reality, but my wife definitely thought I was being highly unrealistic. During those times, I truly believed I was the only one who could keep my mom alive and handle all my clients on almost no sleep, which she saw as just extreme overconfidence about what I could manage alone.\n\nDoctor: When you have these 'wired' periods that cause major problems at work and home, do they typically last for at least seven consecutive days, or do they usually subside in less than a week?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure of the exact number of days those wired periods last. They seem to go on for quite a while before I eventually crash, but I haven't tracked them closely enough to say if it's more or less than a week.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, do you find that they consistently last for at least one full week, or do they typically end before that seven-day mark?\n\nPatient: I'm not completely sure about the exact number of days those wired phases last. They definitely go on for several days at a time where I feel energized and barely sleep, but I've never kept close track of whether it's exactly a full week or slightly less.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember if these 'wired' periods have ever lasted for at least one full week, or do they always settle down before that seven-day mark?\n\nPatient: I honestly can't say for sure if they last a full week, because I tend to completely lose track of time when I'm in that wired state. I'm just constantly on the go and doing things until I eventually crash, so the days all bleed together.\n\nDoctor: When you are in these 'wired' states, have you ever had a period where you felt this way for at least four days in a row, even if you are not sure if it reached a full week?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I get into those wired, high-energy phases, they definitely last for at least four days, and often even a week or more before I eventually crash. These swings have been happening off and on like this for a couple of years now."
